2.11. Difficulties encounter in implementation of the Act: Some of the States have
furnished the details of problems facing in implementing the provision of RBD Act and State
Rules in the State/UT. The brief points given by the State Governments in their annual
report is summarized below:-

 Andhra Pradesh: No fresh sanctions of posts of Statistical staff were made in 2020. Sub
Registrars posts are also vacant in many municipalities. The lack of budget under CRS and

MCCD towards trainings, Publicity, inspection and printing of forms affects the successful
implementation of RBD act in the state.

 Arunachal Pradesh: Inadequate infrastructure is the major problem for
implementation of the Act. Further, due to shortage of vital statistics staff, lack of proper
communication, illiteracy among the villagers, villages being scattered in far flung areas,
etc. caused the issue of under reporting.

 Assam: Lack of awareness to private nursing homes regarding their liabilities on each
event of birth and death. Non availability of CRS fund in state budget for implementation of
RBD Act, 1969. There is shortage of computer and other accessories and skilled personnel
in registration units.

 Goa: Damage caused to old registration record of 1970 to 1980 due to frequent
handling.

 Gujarat: Most of the posts of Taluka/District Statistical Assistants are vacant out of total
sanctioned posts. Registrars are not utilizing the powers vested under the section 15 of the
RBD Act, 1969 regarding corrections and cancellation of entries in birth and death record.
Lack of awareness about the need and importance of registration of births and deaths and
lack of registration procedure knowledge among the public.

 Himachal Pradesh: Local Registrars cum Panchayats Secretaries in concerned
Panchayats are not posting vital events online regularly. There is shortage of manpower in
some of the registration units across the State. There have been no financial assistances
from Center & State Govt. to meet out the expenditure towards Information, Education &
Communication (IEC) and other CRS related activities during the year 2020.

                                                                      27
 Jharkhand: Some difficulties are encounter in implementing the provisions of RBD Act
and Rules in respect of delayed registration provisions. Difficulties in updating master data
in ORGI uniform CRS software at district and block level.

 Karnataka: Inadequacy of staff at all levels. Lack of administrative authority to the Chief
Registrar over registration functionaries.

 Kerala: Pre occupation of Registrars and other functionaries with other duties.
Damage caused to very old registration record. Non registration of large chunk of infant and
child deaths particularly in rural areas. Inclusion of details without care in registration by
the public and request for correction of particulars in the registers as entered in other
documents pertaining to the individuals.

 Madhya Pradesh: Vacant posts of Assistant Statistical Officer, Statistical Clerks and
Clerks in various Municipal Corporations and Municipalities. Lack of control over Notifies in
the rural areas.

 Maharashtra: In rural area due to illiteracy, lack of transportation in hilly areas, and
public unawareness are the hurdles in achieving the goal of 100% registration. The Gram
Sevaks who is Ex-officio Registrar of births and deaths, has to cover many villages due to
vacant posts.

 Mizoram: The geographic location of the State has affected the registration process.

 Nagaland: Inadequate postal, transportation and communication facilities. Lack of
remuneration for CRS functionaries and low level of awareness are major difficulties in the
State.

 Odisha: There is shortage of staff/manpower in the State.

 Punjab: There is a shortage of Staff at every level in the State and associated
departments are facing difficulty in managing various affairs of registration of births and
deaths.

 Telangana: COVID 19 is one of the major cause in under reporting. Lack of Sub
registrars and statistical staff in Municipalities. Large number of vacant posts of Panchayats
Secretaries. Lack of Travelling allowance budget for Statistical personnel working at District
level.

 Tripura: Difficulties encountered in implementing the RBD Act and Rules made
thereunder particularly in case of delayed registration. Issues have been arising related to
online registration of births and deaths. There is shortage of Staff at every level in the State,
shortage of machinery and network also.

                                                                      28
 Uttarakhand: Due to lack of manpower, publicity, remote rural area and training,
difficulties encounter in implementation of RBD Act, 1969. Vacant post in birth-death
registration cadre of Health department.
 Uttar Pradesh: Difficulties in reporting events was faced by general public due to
Covid-19 pandemic. Vacant post of Assistant Statistical officer. Additional Statistical officer
etc.
 West Bengal: As such there is no difficulty in implementation of the act at field level.
However, at time problem was faced in some places due to shortage of manpower.
 Jammu & Kashmir: Lack of Inter Departmental coordination and non-availability of the
online process for general masses.
 Puducherry: No difficulties encountered in implementing RBD Act 1969, since
compulsory Registration of vital events was prevalent during the French Rule.
2.12. Circulars/Guidelines issued by the Office of Registrar General, India: In the
year 2020, a circular/letter on the subject "Extension of period for insertion of the name
in Birth Record ­ Amendment in Rule 10 (1)" was issued to States/UTs (28) wherein
approval of central government was granted under section 30 of the Registration of Birth
and Death (RBD) Act 1969 to amend Rule 10 (1) framed under section 14 of RBD Act, 1969
through which the prescribed time period for insertion of name in birth records was
extended by five more years in cases where the birth is already registered and the time
period for insertion of name is over. Later on, in April, 2021, such approval was given to
remaining States/UTs except Jharkhand and Uttarakhand as the prescribed 15 years period
has not been lapsed in these States. This circular/Letter was issued vide number
1/2/(Rule)/2012-VS(CRS) dated 10-11-2020/21-12-2020/08-04-2021.

                                                     

                                                                      29
                                                   CHAPTER ­ III

                              A BRIEF ANALYSIS OF DATA ON REGISTERED
                                  BIRTHS, DEATHS AND INFANT DEATHS

3.1 Births and deaths are the two most important vital events in the life of an individual and for
the society as a whole. Based on the data collected under the Civil Registration System on
registered births and deaths, this chapter presents the trend in number of registered events and
other related indicators, at the State and National level.
3.2 In 2020, the number of registration units along with the level of reporting at the national
level and the States/UTs with rural/urban details may be seen at Statement 5. It may be noted here
that level of reporting indicates the status of returns of registered births and deaths by the
concerned Registrar within a State / UT during the year. It provides the deficiency at a glance and
helps in reviewing the registration system and taking measures that would be necessary to
improve the registration levels, wherever required.
3.3 At the national level, 96.0% of the registration units reported monthly returns of registered
births and deaths to the District Registrar's or Chief Registrar's Office during 2020. For rural areas,
the reporting level are at 96.0% and for urban areas, it is 96.3%. However, these figures do not
include Arunachal Pradesh and Delhi as these States/UTs have provided only partial information.
3.4 Cent percent level of reporting have been achieved by 20 States/UTs viz. Andhra Pradesh,
Assam, Chhattisgarh, Goa, Gujarat, Haryana, Karnataka, Kerala, Mizoram, Punjab, Rajasthan, Sikkim,
Tamil Nadu, Tripura, A & N Islands, Chandigarh, D & N Haveli and Daman & Diu, Delhi,
Lakshadweep and Puducherry.

3.6 The above data provides details of the number of registered births and deaths over the years.
The number of registered births has decreased from 2.48 crores in 2019 to 2.42 crores in 2020 ­ a
decrease of about 2.4%. In the case of registered births, there has been a fall in the year 2020 in almost all
States/UTs except Bihar, Haryana, Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan, Sikkim, A& N Islands, Jammu & Kashmir,
Ladakh compared to 2019. Eleven States/UTs namely Uttar Pradesh, Telangana, Gujarat, Jharkhand,
Delhi, Karnataka, Chhattisgarh, Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra, Kerala and West Bengal have contributed
significantly to the decrease in Registered Births in 2020 over 2019.

                                                                            36
3.9 Registration of Birth & Death within prescribed time limit of 21 days

3.9.1 The registered births and deaths have been classified in four categories depending upon their
period of registration. The four time periods considered for the purpose are: i) within prescribed time
limit (up to 21 days), ii) After 21 days but within 30 days, iii) After 30 days but within 1 year, iv) More
than 1 year. For 2020, Thirty-three (33) States/UTs have provided data on Births and Deaths
Registration within prescribed time period (i.e within 21 days). In respect of both Time Gap Birth and
Death, partial information has been submitted by 3 States /UTs namely, Maharashtra, Sikkim and Delhi
and therefore have not been taken into account while consolidating the data for Time gap in
Registration of Births & Deaths tables.

3.9.2 On the basis of data received from States/UTs the percentage of registration achieved out of
total registration undertaken in 2020 within prescribed time limit (21 days) is as under:

Statement 10: Distribution of States/UTs by Registration completed within the
                                prescribed time limit (21 days)

                       Chandigarh, Lakshadweep , Mizoram,    Haryana, West Bengal, A & N Islands,
                                                            Puducherry, Himachal Pradesh, Tamil
                           D & N Haveli and Daman & Diu,     Nadu, Andhra Pradesh, Gujarat (11)

3.9.3 The above Statement 10 shows the percentage of births registered within the prescribed time-
period of 21 days to the total births registered in the state/UT. 15 States/UTs have achieved more than
90% registration of births within the prescribed time limit of 21 days. Further, 2 States/UTs are in the
category of more than 80 to less than or equal to 90 percent, 9 States are in the category of more than
50 to less than or equal to 80 percent and, the remaining 7 States are under the category of less than or
equal to 50 percent in completing the birth registration within the time limit of 21 days. In Statement
11, the State-wise percentage of birth registered within the prescribed time period has been arranged
in descending order.

3.9.4 Statement 10 also shows the percentage of deaths registered within the prescribed time period
of 21 days to the total deaths registered in the state and arranged in descending order. 11 States/UTs
have achieved more than 90% registration of death within the prescribed time limit of 21 days. 7
States/UTs are in the category of more than 80 to less than or equal to 90 percent, 7 States are in the
category of more than 50 to less than or equal to 80 percent, and the remaining 8 States are under the
category of less than or equal to 50 percent in death registration within the prescribed time limit of 21

3.11. Sex-wise Distribution of Registered Births and Deaths of Major States

3.11.1 Chart 7 and 8 respectively shows the sex-wise distribution of registered births and deaths for
the major States (population of more than 1 Crore as per Census 2011). The data on birth and death
registrations reveals that the share of male registration is more than the female in almost all these
major States. This gap is much wider in case of Birth registration in Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, Gujarat,
Haryana, Jharkhand, Maharashtra and Rajasthan. While in case of Death Registration, the gap is wider in
almost all major States viz. Jharkhand, Haryana, Madhya Pradesh, Bihar, Rajasthan, Karnataka and Uttar
Pradesh. The State-wise figures have been provided in Statement 14.

3.12 Medical attention received at the time of birth and death

3.12.1 The complete information for 2020 in respect of birth by type of medical attention received at
the time of delivery has been reported from only 34 States/UTs. As partial information has been
submitted by two States namely, Maharashtra and Sikkim, their data has not been taken into account
while consolidating the data. The distribution of medical attention received at the time of birth for these
States/UTs has been illustrated in Chart 9. While the data shows that majority of births have been
occurred in an institution, either in Government or private hospitals. Institutional births occurred in
about 73.7% of cases, 6.5% of the cases were attended by the Physicians/Nurse/Midwife. However,
about 11.3% of the events are still attended by untrained Mid-wife/Dai and another 8.4% by others
and Not stated/Not Available together.

3.12.2 Complete information on Medical Attention received by deceased at the time of death has been
received from 34 States/UTs. Two States namely Maharashtra and Sikkim have submitted only partial
data and therefore their data has not been used while consolidating the data. Chart 10 represents the
distribution of medical attention received by the deceased at the time of death. It is evident from Chart
that 28.0% of the total registered deaths have occurred in institutions and is on a higher side than that
of other places from where the deceased had received medical attention. About 16.4% of Registered
Deaths were reported under Medical Attention Other than Institutions. About 1.3% of the total
registered deaths during 2020 have received medical attention from qualified allopathic professional
and practitioner of other system together and 45.0% of deceased did not receive any medical attention
at the time of death.

3.13 Infant Deaths

3.13.1 The infant deaths registered by place of occurrence (Rural /Urban) of the event for the year
2020 has been given in Statement 15. It may be observed that the share of rural area is only 23.4%
while that of urban area is 76.6% in total registered infant deaths during the year. Non-registration of
infant deaths in rural area is a cause of concern which may be due to non-reporting of infant deaths to
the Registrars especially in case of domiciliary events.

3.14 Still Births

3.14.1 The World Health Organization (WHO) defines a still birth as a baby born with no signs of life at
or after 28 weeks' gestation. Still birth, also known as foetal death, has been defined as `death prior to
the complete expulsion or extraction from its mother of a product of human conception, irrespective of the
duration of pregnancy and which is not an induced termination of pregnancy. The death is indicated by the
fact that after such expulsion or extraction, the fetus does not breathe or show any other evidence of life,
such as beating of the heart, pulsation of the umbilical cord, or definite movement of voluntary muscles.
Heartbeats are to be distinguished from transient cardiac contractions; stillbirths are to be distinguished
from fleeting respiratory efforts or gasps.'

3.14.2 Statement 17 presents still births registered by place of occurrence (Rural /Urban) of the event
for the year 2020 and it may be observed that in total registered still births, share of rural registered
still births is only 40.8% which may be due to poor netting of stillbirths in rural areas. In urban areas,
this share is about 57.9% which may be due to better netting of still births from medical institutions.
State/UT-wise registered still births have been depicted in Table 4.

             Statement 17: Still Births registered during 2020

States namely Maharashtra, Sikkim and Delhi have provided only partial data on Time Gap Births and
Deaths Registration and therefore not included.
